  2. **Every commit that stores records released from the hold moves the saved position past them in that same transaction, but only within the run the position belongs to** (D6 on `f7971f82`). The node keeps one position for a sensor: the highest run accepted (its counter and `run_id`) and T in it. A seq counts within one run.
     - **A released record of the position's own run:** T moves to the higher of the T already saved and the highest seq released. Item 1 makes that valid, since everything below each released record is already durable.
     - **A released record of an earlier run** never changes T. That happens on `main` in the ordinary course: an engine restart starts a new run, the node saves the new run with T = 0 before it accepts its hello, and the old run's held record is released by `Link.giveWay`, `Result` or `TickHold`. **If the release commits it,** it is stored as the earlier run's record, the cursor written in that commit is the position unchanged, and nothing is counted: a committed record is neither refused nor dropped. No resend of it can come, since the sensor can no longer offer that run (`run-regressed`), so it cannot be stored twice. **If the release refuses or drops it,** that count goes into the refused or dropped total of the run the cursor now holds, the newer run's, the only totals the cursor carries (CH-18); status names the run it came from. **Nothing of the new run is dropped:** its records at or below the old run's seq are read against the new run's own T.
     - This holds for `Link.Result`'s release, `Link.TickHold`'s deadline release, `Link.giveWay`, and `Link.RecoverHold`'s commit at start (`commitBatch`, `release`, `hold.go`; the cursor value in `Link.record`, `link.go`). The store writes the batch's cursor in the same transaction as its records (`platform/store/commit.go`; D6). **`RecoverHold` also compares the saved position,** not the stream's head alone, so that a release which stored nothing but moved the position is not run again after a second kill (D6's note).
  3. **A record the release does not store has its count in the same step.** One that the unresolved budget drops, or that the record cap or C13 refuses, has its total raised in the cursor written by that release's commit. If the whole release stores nothing, a `SaveSourceCursor` carries the totals and the new position before any `ack` passes them (ruling 2 on #627). A store error that is not final (D5-1) leaves the records held, the position unmoved, and the release is tried again at the next tick.
  4. **The duplicate check uses the position saved now, not the one at the hello.** A record whose seq is at or below the run's saved T at the moment it is read is a duplicate: dropped, counted under `duplicate`, never stored. A release in mid-connection can move T above L, the highest seq read on the connection, but only past restored held records. So a resend of n is a duplicate whether it arrives before the release (the hold drops it, matched by `run_id` and `seq`, as `HeldRecord` carries them) or after (its seq is at or below T). **No `ack` names a seq above both the highest seq the node has read on the connection and the latest tick's `sent_seq`** (D3's note; CH-17): when a release raises T above both, the `ack` names the higher of the two figures (the highest seq read and the latest `sent_seq`) until the reads catch up, CH-17's words, so it never makes the agent close on an `ack` above what it sent (D4's note 1 on #633; D5's N1 on #634).
  5. **At start,** `RecoverHold` runs before any connection (`startSensor`, `sensor.go`), so the first `node-hello`'s `taken` already includes every record it released.
  - So **no committed record is ever above the `taken` sent in `node-hello`, and no record is stored twice, in either order.**
- **What is volatile:** only the split of the totals by reason and by asset, which the `intake.drop-counted` event carries (§1.9 item 5). A crash loses the split since the last such event; the totals survive.
- **A second copy of the run** (counter and `run_id`) is in `sensorrun.json`, written at each **new** run accepted, not at each resume, so a lost network database cannot bring back an old run as new. At start the node takes the higher copy; equal counters with different `run_id`s are a fork (`fork-held`). A copy read from a file that is behind (§1.1) can only be lower, so taking the higher stays safe.
- **A sensor database lost where a position existed** (SL-31; D4's B5 on #633). The node takes a database missing at open under a live enrolment as lost, whatever `sensorrun.json` holds (§1.1, D6's B2 on #641); the run file then says only whether a copy of the run exists. It creates the database, after it has written the run file with that sensor's pairs marked spent (§1.1, SL-30 at `41a073f2`), and holds no D, no spans, no `prior`, no digest, no baselines and no W. Status names the lost position, says that the position is taken from the run file with the database lost, and says that a `prior` owed in it may be lost. **The hello** (D1's ruling, comment 6837755868 on #644; SL-31 at `41a073f2`, which states the same):
  - **When the run file holds a copy of the run** (current or behind: with the database gone its `write_no` cannot be checked, and a copy can only be lower than the truth), `node-hello` names that run with `taken.seq` 0, CH-6's form for a run with nothing taken. The agent resumes it from the oldest record it still holds, or, if it no longer holds that run, does not apply `taken` and sends its true hello (CH-6). **Without a copy,** `node-hello` carries no `taken`, and the agent's oldest run arrives as a new run.
  - **The copy carries its generation, and SL-R6's rule applies to it** (D3's condition): a copy from below the generation at which the node's position began (§1.3) reads as none. Without this, after a `rekey` the agent's run 1 would meet the old copy's counter and close as `run-regressed`.
  - *Why a `taken` from the copy, and not none:* the node takes the copy as its saved run, so that a lost database cannot bring back an old run as new. With that copy saved and no `taken` sent, the agent would offer the same run as a new run with `prior`, and CH-13 row 5 (equal counter and `run_id`) would close it as `hello` at every dial; dropping the copy would let a replayed older run in as new. It needs no change to CH-13.
  - **What a stale copy costs** (D3's bound, adopted by D1): a copy of the sensor or a restored snapshot whose counter lies between the stale copy's and the true one is accepted where it should be refused as `run-regressed`, for that window only and only after two faults on the node. A compromised sensor gains nothing it could not already do (SL-16's residual). **A second cost: a `sensor.run-loss` written twice** (SL-31's `prior` paragraph at #641's `467c1dd6`; D6's N8 on #641): with a copy that is behind, the node can judge as new a run whose `sensor.run-loss` it already wrote, and write it again. The status that names the position as taken from the run file names that repeat as possible, and the event's generation, counter and `run_id` let an upstream reader tell it.
  - **SL-30's counts read as spent,** for an answered pair and an unanswered one alike (§1.1): with the database gone the run file cannot be checked against it, and the cursor's proof is gone with it. So the dial that would send this hello waits for an administrator's re-arm (§1.1), which fails closed, and `sensor-dial-limit` stands meanwhile.
  - Test: `TestLostDatabaseHello` (the database deleted with a current run file: no dial until a re-arm, then `node-hello` names the copy at seq 0, the resume is accepted, and status names the position as taken from the run file; with the copy from generation 1 and the position begun at generation 2 by a `rekey`: no `taken`, and the agent's run 1 is accepted as a new run; controls: a copy taken whatever its generation, which closes run 1 as `run-regressed`; a node that keeps the copy and sends no `taken`, which closes the hello at every dial; and a node that reads the counts from the unchecked file, which dials without a re-arm).

  Either way the hello's spans are new against a D of 0, admitted only with the rise the sensor states, and each opens a `lost` gap cut at its receipt (CH-40). **Limit, stated:** the records the agent trimmed after the lost acknowledgements count in G at the first resume, so `sensor-gap-undeclared` is raised once, and status names the lost position as its likely cause. That is loud, and it trusts nothing the node no longer holds.
- **On a `renew`'s first resume** (CH-13 row 5b; ruling 1 on #633 and #634 (comment 6805936000)). Row 5b's condition: the hello is a resume, its `run_counter` and `run_id` equal the saved position's, and its generation is higher than the saved position's, which only a `renew` with the pin unchanged can give (CH-12). **Row 5b applies only when the node carried the position over to the live enrolment's generation, which it does for a `renew` with the pin unchanged and for nothing else; any other resume under a higher generation closes, `hello`** (CH-13 row 5). **Before it accepts that hello, the node saves the new generation in the cursor,** T, G, D and the totals unchanged. A save that fails closes the connection with `position-not-saved` and raises `sensor-position-not-saved`, as at a new run. That save is also SL-30's proof for the renewed pair (§1.1).
- **At a new run** (CH-13 row 6) the cursor is saved with T = 0 **before the hello is accepted**, and so before the first record is taken. **Before that save, the node runs CH-15's check of G against D for the run the cursor still holds** (D5's N9 on #634), since the save overwrites G and D. **`prior` is durable at the node** (CH-16): its counts, spans, `previous_end` and `started_by` are saved **in the cursor, in row 6's same `SaveSourceCursor`, with the run's `config_digest`** (SL-31; D3's preference), each with an owed mark. `prior` stays there until the `sensor.run-loss` event that carries it commits to the health stream, and only a later cursor save clears the mark, the order `SaveSourceCursor`'s own comment gives for a health event (`platform/store/outbox.go`). The two writes are in two databases, so a kill between them gives the event twice, never not at all: **the event carries the run's generation, counter and `run_id`,** so an upstream reader can see one declaration twice, and status counts the owed events written at a start as possibly second copies. A node crash, an event held past the ceiling, or a run file that is lost or behind does not lose the sensor's declaration; only a lost sensor database can, and status then names it. A save that fails closes the connection with `position-not-saved`, is counted, and raises `sensor-position-not-saved`.
- **The hello checks** are CH-13's table: `binding`, `run-regressed`, `run-forked`, resume, new run, `pinned_by`, the digest and the buffer floor. On a resume, `prior` must be absent and `drops_channel` must not be below the D the node holds for the run, else `hello`; and row 5a, a resume whose `newest_seq` is below the node's taken seq, is `run-regressed`. **CH-14 changes `platform/sensorlink`'s order:** a record that decodes strictly takes its `seq` before any refusal, `sensor-id` included, so no false gap is counted. `run-forked` writes `fork-held` to `sensors.json` and stops the dialer until a new enrolment with a new key, or a revocation (SL-16).
- **A store error that is not final** (drain-only, a closed database, an I/O fault) is not taken: the link closes the connection with `store`, T does not move, and the dialer backs off, so the sensor's buffer holds the records (D5-1). This replaces `Link.storeDrop`'s `DropStore` branch, which today drops and counts them (`link.go`).
- **G against D:** `sensor-gap-undeclared` is raised once for each rise of the excess in a run (CH-15), as a status alarm, with its health events under the cap of §1.9 item 5 (D4's point 5; D3's note 3 on #627). A declared loss writes its health event from the rise of D, whichever message carried it (D3's note 4 on #627).
- **Tests**, in `platform/sensorlink`: `TestHelloRunRegressed`, `TestHelloRunForked`, `TestHelloBinding`; `TestPositionSavedBeforeFirstRecord` (control: saved after, which after a kill reads as a fork); `TestResumeNoDuplicate` (killed after a commit and before the `ack`: the resend is a duplicate; control: a `taken` below a committed record, which stores it twice; and the legs killed after a hold save and before the next cursor save: (i) the resend arrives while n is held and is dropped by the hold; (ii) the hold's deadline passes before the resume, `TickHold` commits n, and the resend is a duplicate by the saved T; (iii) the kill inside a release, `RecoverHold` commits n at start, and the first `taken` includes n; (iv) a restored record that the budget drops at its release has its total in that release's cursor; (v) an engine restart while run R holds n, and n released after run R + 1's hello: R + 1's T does not move, and its records with a seq at or below n are stored; in (i) to (iv), n is stored once; controls: for (v), the move applied whatever the run, which drops R + 1's records as duplicates; controls: the duplicate match against held records only, and a release commit that does not move the position, each of which stores n twice; and the cursor not saved before the hold save, under which moving T past n loses a refusal's count); `TestAckNeverAboveCursor` (control: an `ack` from memory, which after a kill has acknowledged a record whose count is gone); `TestTotalsSurviveCrash` (a flood of drops, then a kill: the totals in the cursor equal the drops; control: totals in the minute event only); `TestPositionMonotoneOnRelease`; `TestStoreErrorNotTaken` (control: `main`'s `DropStore`); **H-7** (CH-13 row 5b): a `renew` with the pin unchanged, the first hello under the new generation a resume, and the cursor holding the new generation before the hello is accepted; with `sensorrun.json` then unreadable at a start, the cursor proves the renewed pair and the node redials at the ordinary rule; a save made to fail closes `position-not-saved` (control: a node that saves nothing on a resume, under which the renewed pair reads as unproven and the link stops with `sensor-dial-limit`). **`TestRunFileRolledBack`, D1's alarm half** (D4-90), in `platform/node/sensortest` on the test clock: a run with `sensor-transmitted` standing, a `prior` whose `sensor.run-loss` has not committed, a digest, two dials made to a second pair that has not answered, and a spent re-arm; the node stopped, `sensorrun.json` replaced by its copy from before those writes, and the node started; in a second run the file deleted, and in a third made unreadable. In each: `sensor-transmitted` stands until an administrator clears it; the unanswered pair gets no further dial and `sensor-dial-limit` stands; the re-arm request file put back is refused; the `prior` and the digest are those of the cursor, and the `sensor.run-loss` is written once or twice, never not at all; the held events of the file are named in status as possibly lost; and `sensor-state-unread` stands with cause `run-file`. *Controls:* a node that trusts a readable file (no `write_no`), which clears `sensor-transmitted` and dials the pair twice more; and `prior` and the digest kept in `sensorrun.json` as at `72f09c81`, under which the old file brings back a `prior` already written (a second event) and an earlier run's digest. **Its fourth run, SL-T24 (g)'s third run** (D6's B4 on #641; SL-T24 at `467c1dd6`), on a node with one sensor: the sensor database deleted and `sensorrun.json` replaced by its copy from before the raise, so that nothing can show the file to be behind: `sensor-transmitted` stands before any heartbeat (control: a node that applies the lost-database rule to SL-30's counts only, which clears it). **Its fifth run, SL-T24 (g)'s fourth run** (D6's N5 on #641 at `467c1dd6`; SL-T24 at `da7a84ee`): the sensor database kept and its cursor removed: the alarm stands (control: a node that reads only an absent database as lost, which clears it). **SL-T24's legs (a) to (d)**, in `platform/sensorlink` for (a) to (c) and `platform/node/sensortest` for (d), which needs the health stream (D5's N9 on #641). On the rig: SL-T9 (a) to (k), and (g) with the fork hold after a node restart.

## 1.5 Clock skew (brief item 4)

**Why it matters** (`desk`). The hold ties a flow whose start, on the sensor's clock, falls within the session's start and end, on the node host's clock, widened by `g` = `DefaultTieGraceS`, 30 s (`hold.go` `Result`). The detector's clock is the latest `first_utc` it has read, and it judges a session once that clock passes its end plus `g` (`netdetect` `Observe`, `judge`).

**The measure** (CH-22 as ruled). At each `sensor-hello` and each accepted `tick`, never at a heartbeat or a record, the node takes `o` = its wall time at receipt minus the message's `sensor_utc`. The delay in `o` is never negative. A tick that arrives less than `TickMinSpacing` (5 s) after the previous accepted tick on the connection, other than the first after the hello, is counted under `tick_early` and does no further work: no skew sample, no gap check, no timer reset (difference 10 on #627; CH-8).

- **The alarm** uses `E`, the smallest `o` of the connection's last 6 ticks: raised when |E| is above 2,000 ms, cleared at 1,500 ms or less (difference 5 on #627; CH-22).
- **The tie** uses the latest sample too: **no tie is made while the latest `o` is outside 2,000 ms,** even before the alarm's minimum catches up (D3's L2 (iii)). So a clock stepped back ties nothing from its first tick.
- **The sign, and which comparisons use it** (D4's finding 1 on `4d03075a`; D4, D6). The convention is CH-22's: `o` = the node's wall time at receipt − the message's `sensor_utc`, so a positive `o`, and a positive E, means the sensor's clock is behind the node's. **A sensor time is brought to the node's clock by adding E:** `t_node = t_sensor + E`. *Worked example:* the sensor runs 1.5 s behind the node and the network adds 0.01 s, so `o` = E = +1.51 s; a flow the sensor stamps `10:00:00.000` happened at about `10:00:01.51` on the node's clock, which is the time the node compares with a session's start and end. (Subtracting E, as the text at `4d03075a`, D3's text and the first wording of D1's short-break ruling had it, moves the time by 2E the wrong way, a 4 s error at the 2,000 ms bound; D1 corrected that wording on #627, comment 6803089547.) While neither the alarm nor the latest sample is outside the bound, the node's tie comparisons (`Result`'s window, part (d)'s expiry, the late index, and §1.6's `made_utc` check) use `t_sensor + E`. **Nothing stored is re-timed:** capture times stay as the sensor wrote them. While the skew stands, no comparison is made, because no tie is made.
- **No tie across a move of either clock** (SL-17 at #641's `e164e416`; D6's B1 on #641 at `c0cf1ecc`, which D1 accepted, with a second half by D3, keyed on capture time for D6's B1 on #641 at `50a67730`; D5's point on #641 at `c0cf1ecc`). The alarm compares the two clocks, so it cannot see a step that one time source makes on both, nor a correction of the node's clock alone once the alarm has stood: after either the two agree, while the times above were stored on the old clock. Two halves, and a rule on when a tie is decided:
  - **A stored time ties only while the node's clock has not moved since the node recorded it.** With each time a tie may compare (`Result`'s window, part (d)'s expiry, a late-index entry, a break's two ends), the node keeps the offset of its wall clock from its monotonic clock when it recorded that time, and compares a sensor time with it only while that offset has moved by no more than `max_skew_ms` since, end to end and not by the steps found, so small steps that add up count. A run's window is the collector's `started_at` and `ended_at`, which the node does not see read: it keeps its offset at `Dispatch` and at `Result`, both must agree with the current one, and the two times must lie between the node's own wall-clock times at those two moments, within `max_skew_ms`. **A time recorded before the node's start ties nothing.** These refusals have no clock behind them: status counts them apart as refused for a node start, not as a clock move (item 5 below; D6's N3 on #641 at `50a67730`).
  - **A record ties only if it was captured after the last move of the sensor's clock against the node's monotonic clock** (SL-17 at #641's `e164e416`; D6's B1 on #641 at `50a67730`: numbering is not capture, so a flow captured before a move and numbered after the tick that shows it tied, displaced by the move). At each tick and each hello the node takes K, the `sensor_utc` stated less the node's monotonic time at its receipt. K's baseline is its value at the first hello after the node's start, or at the last change, and **K and its baseline are kept for each sensor across its connections while the node runs**, so a move made inside a break is seen at the next hello. When K differs from its baseline by more than `max_skew_ms`, the node raises the sensor's capture floor to the highest K since the baseline plus the node's monotonic time at that receipt plus `max_skew_ms`, and takes that K as the baseline; the floor is held in memory, one value for each sensor, and never falls while the node runs. **No record whose capture time is at or before the floor takes a tie,** whenever it was numbered and whenever it is read. A step of the node's clock alone moves E with it, and placed times stay right; a step of the sensor's clock, alone or with the node's, displaces every record captured before it, however late the engine writes it. After a step back of Δ, ties of captures on the new clock are refused for about Δ + `max_skew_ms`.
  - **A tie is decided no earlier than the receipt of the first tick read after its record,** with K, the floor, the alarm and the latest sample as they stand then (SL-17; D6's N1 on #641 at `50a67730`). A record that would tie waits for that tick, in the hold where one holds it, and is committed untied under `clock-skew` if the connection ends first or the tick does not come within `HeartbeatGapMax` of its read. A tie waits up to one tick interval, and a move between two ticks is seen before any tie it could displace.
  - **How the node builds the wait** (D5, for the rule above). A run's held flows that would tie at `Result` stay in the hold, as a failed dial's do (`HoldWaiting`, `hold.go`), until that tick, and the run's `after_seq` follows their release, so the detector is handed the session only after them. A record that would tie through the late index or the open table, parts (e) and (d), is read after its session was handed over, so it enters the hold as a waiting record, with the position saved through n − 1 first (§1.4), at most `HoldRecordCap` (23) such records at once, past which one is committed untied under `clock-skew`. **While such a record waits, the detector's drive (§1.9 item 3) reads no observation committed after it was read,** so the late index's order, which [S2] states, holds in what the detector reads. K, its baseline and the floor are memory only, in the sensor manager's state for the sensor, beside the late index; nothing of them is in the cursor or in `sensorrun.json`. The cost: a tie, and the hand-over of its session, up to one tick interval later (10 s, and at most `HeartbeatGapMax`, 30 s, before the record commits untied); the detector's reading, and so every alert of that sensor, up to the same while a late or open-table tie waits; one hold save and one cursor save for each such record; and up to 1.5 MiB of waiting records beside a run's hold, under the configuration record cap. *Test:* `TestTieWaitHoldsDetector` (a late-index tie waiting for its tick while the source writes flows that start past its session's end plus `g`: the session is judged with the tie, and raises no `own-session-unseen`; control: a drive that reads past the waiting record, which judges the session unseen).
  - A backlog record refused at the break's ends is judged by §1.6, under `sensor-records-late`, and so is a record that the break's ends and another rule here both refuse, with `node.backlog: true` and no `node.skewed` (D6's N5 on #641 at `50a67730`). Any other flow refused under either half, or for want of its tick, is committed untied with `node.skewed: true`, and a run whose window is refused is marked `skewed`, so their alerts come under `clock-skew` (item 5 below). A run committed before the move cannot be marked, since the store is append-only: its own session's flow still alerts under `clock-skew`, aggregated, and its `own-session-unseen` comes per asset, not aggregated, within the late index's 15 minutes before the move (D6's N3 on #641 at `50a67730`). It fails closed: with no move nothing changes; after one, the sessions recorded before it and the records captured before it whose tie is decided after it alert, aggregated. What the route takes is control of a time source that serves both hosts (O-10), or of the node host; what stays is in AP-35, among it a step back made while the node is stopped, against a flow held open across the stop whose record the engine writes after the first hello (A6), since nothing of K survives a start.
- **Bound: 2,000 ms,** an order below `g`. **Assumption:** a site clock under NTP on a LAN keeps well within a second. The owner has not chosen the time source (O-10), so this is unshown (§1.14).

**While no tie is made for skew** (SL-17). **One rule for both of its causes** (D4's B3 on #633): the alarm standing, or the latest sample outside `max_skew_ms` while E is not, as after a clock stepped back or a tick read late. Items 2 to 5 apply alike to both; item 1 to the alarm only.

1. `sensor-clock-skew` in status, repeated hourly, and its health events under the cap (§1.9 item 5).
2. **No tie is made:** `Dispatch` holds nothing for that sensor, every run it holds gives way untied at the onset (as `giveWay` does), and the late index ties nothing.
3. **The alerts are aggregated** (ruling 13 on #628; difference 4 on #627; CH-41). An observation committed while no tie is made for skew carries `node.skewed: true`, and `collection.run` of a session ended then carries `skewed`. The detector puts their `own-session-unseen` and `own-session-outside-record` alerts under one key for each rule and sensor, `asset_id` `clock-skew`. **The aggregated alert names the assets it covers, up to 8** (as `SensorDropDetailAssets`, `sensor.go`), enrolled ones first, **and counts the rest.** With C13 §7.3's coalescing that is at most 4 records every 300 s for each sensor, whatever the schedule; without it, up to 1,024 a window at 256 run endpoints (`MaxDetectorEndpoints`).
4. **A flow that matches no session even at |E| plus `g` keeps its asset's own key** (D6's question 5): the aggregation covers only flows that would have tied but for the skew, so a spoofed session that matches no run still alerts on its own.
5. **Nothing is suppressed.** Every flow and session is judged. **The key `clock-skew` means "no tie was made for skew"**, the alarm, the latest sample or the rule on the two clocks (above), and not that the alarm stood; status counts the windows of each cause apart (`skew_alarm_windows`, `skew_latest_windows`, and a count of its own, `skew_moved_windows`, for a tie refused under the rule on the two clocks, and one more, `skew_prestart_windows`, for a tie refused because its time was recorded before the node's start), so an aggregated alert with no alarm standing is explained there.
6. **A sample read after the node's own read loop was held** (D4's B3 on #633). o is taken at the node's read, and the read loop takes the next frame only after each commit (F4), so a commit held 3 s, F-016's figure on the hosted Windows runner, can put an honest sensor's latest sample above 2,000 ms. **Such a sample still gates the tie,** which fails closed (no tie, the observations marked and aggregated), and is **counted** under `skew_samples_after_hold` when the read-loop step before it lasted longer than `AckMaxDelay` (1 s), so a slow node shows as the cause in status. It still enters E, whose least of six is not raised by one late sample. **The same hold moves K** (the rule on the two clocks, above; D6's N2 on #641 at `50a67730`): K falls by the hold and returns at the next tick, two changes, each raising the capture floor and refusing the ties not yet decided. Such a change at a tick read after a held loop is counted under `skew_samples_after_hold`, not as a clock move. On Windows K may drift with the oscillator's error, about 2 s in 11 h at 50 ppm, since Go's monotonic reading there is the interrupt time, which the time service does not steer (an inference, not verified); each crossing of `max_skew_ms` refuses the ties pending then.

**Limit** (D6): skew can be caused from the management network, by delaying ticks or forging an unauthenticated time source (O-10). The aggregation then covers the window, and item 4 keeps a flow that matches no session visible. **A source that serves both hosts** moves both clocks together, which the alarm cannot see; the rule on the two clocks (above) is the check then (SL-17 at #641's `50a67730`; D6's B1 on #641 at `c0cf1ecc`). Recorded for AP-35 (D3).

**Tests.** `TestSkewEstimateTakesLeastDelay`; `TestSkewCorrectionAddsE` (a sensor 1.5 s behind: a flow at the session's start ties; control: `t_sensor − E`, which pushes it 3 s out of a window narrowed for the test); `TestSkewAlarmHysteresis` (2,000 ms and 1,500 ms); `TestSkewIgnoresHeartbeat`; `TestSkewStepBackTiesNothing` (control: ties gated on E, which ties during the first minute); `TestSkewLatestSampleMarks` (D4's B3: a latest sample above 2,000 ms with E below, from a stepped clock and from a read loop held 3 s by a commit seam: the observations committed then carry `node.skewed`, their own-session alerts come under `clock-skew`, `sensor-clock-skew` is not raised, and status counts `skew_latest_windows` and, for the held read, `skew_samples_after_hold`; control: marking only while the alarm stands, under which the flows commit untied and unmarked and alert per asset with no cause shown); `TestSkewTiesNothing`; `TestTieStoredTimeAfterNodeStep` (on the test clock, ticks every 10 s, `tie_grace_s` 30 s, each dial that fails putting its session in the late index: a dial to a relay that fails, recorded as [10:00:00, 10:00:10]; at 10:00:30 the test source's clock and the node's stepped back 300 s together; at 10:05:00, true time, a Telnet session from the node's address to that relay, stamped 10:00:00: committed untied with `node.skewed: true`, its `own-session-outside-record` alert under `clock-skew`; control: a node with no first half, which compares `t_sensor + E` with the stored window as it is and ties it; second case (reworked for D5's point on #641 at `50a67730`: with the alarm standing no window was stored, and the control tied nothing either), `tie_grace_s` 60 s: the node's clock and the source's both 300 s ahead of true time, so no alarm stands, and a failing dial recorded as [X − 10 s, X]; 2 s after X the node's clock alone stepped back 300 s, which raises the alarm, and 2 s later the source's clock too, after which the alarm clears; 340 s after the dial's end, true time, a Telnet session from the node's address to that relay, placed at about X + 40 s, past the capture floor at about X + 16 s: untied, under `clock-skew`; control: the same node, which ties it; the same correction made on both clocks together in steps of 1,900 ms, one every 2 s of monotonic time, from 2 s after X, the test clock running the step check every second so that no check spans two steps (D6's N5 on #641 at `50a67730`), with the session 345 s after the dial's end, placed at about X + 45 s, past the floor at about X + 24 s: untied; control: a node that compares only the steps its check finds, which finds none and ties it; third case, the node and the source stopped together for one minute and both clocks stepped back five minutes during the stop, after a failing dial that ended 30 s before the stop; after the start and the backlog, a Telnet session from the node's address to that relay placed inside that session's window: untied; control: a node that ties by the late index as it rebuilds it at a start); `TestTieRecordBeforeSensorStep` (the source's clock 300 s ahead of the node's, `sensor-clock-skew` standing; a Telnet session from the node's address to a relay at T, true time, stamped T + 300 s; the source's clock stepped back 300 s at T + 5 s, and that session's `start` record, numbered and synced before the first tick after the correction, sent at T + 305 s behind a queue of records, ticks on time; a dial to that relay that fails, at T + 300 s for 2 s: the record, placed at T + 300 s inside the run's window, is committed untied under `clock-skew`; control: a node with the first half alone, which ties it; second case, both clocks true until T + 5 s and then stepped back 300 s together, the session stamped T, the dial at T + 300 s true time: untied; control: the same node, which ties it; third case, a record written late (D6's B1 on #641 at `50a67730`): the first case with the session held open until after the dial and its `start` record written at its end, numbered after the first tick that shows the correction: untied, under `clock-skew`; control: a node that refuses by numbering, no record numbered at or before the `newest_seq` of the tick that shows the move, which ties it; fourth case, a move inside a break: the source's clock 300 s ahead, a Telnet session at T stamped T + 300 s and held open, the connection reset at T + 10 s, the source's clock stepped back 300 s at T + 20 s, the next hello accepted at T + 40 s, a failing dial to that relay at T + 300 s for 2 s, the session closed after it and its `start` record written at its end, numbered after the hello's `newest_seq` and read once the backlog is through: untied, under `clock-skew`; control: a node that takes K's baseline afresh at each hello, which ties it); `TestTieWaitsForTick` (D6's N1 on #641 at `50a67730`: the two clocks in agreement; a failing dial to a relay recorded as [D, D + 2 s]; 300 s later, 3 s after a tick's receipt, the source's clock alone stepped back 300 s; 1 s after the step a Telnet session from the node's address to that relay, placed at about D + 1 s and read 1 s later, before the next tick: committed untied under `clock-skew` at the next tick; control: a node that decides a tie at its record's read, with the latest sample from before the step, which ties it); and D4's `TestSkewAlertsAggregated` (50 sessions give at most 4 records a window and name 8 assets with a count of the rest; control: keyed by asset, at least 50) and `TestSkewUnmatchedFlowKeepsKey` (control: every flow of the window aggregated, which puts a flow that matches no run under `clock-skew`, D3). On the rig: the test source states a `sensor_utc` offset by 10 s, since a time namespace cannot move a namespace's wall clock (D6, not read), and the alarm is raised within one tick of the hello.

## 1.6 Records after a break, and late records (brief item 5)

**What happens today** (`desk`). The hold watches a run's endpoint from `Dispatch` until `Result`, `Abandon` or its deadline (`hold.go`). While the link is down nothing is held, so `Result` releases at once. When the backlog arrives, the session's records find no holding run (`runFor`) and no open entry, and commit untied (`Link.observe`, `commitOne`). An untied record never marks its session seen (`netdetect` `track`), so the session raises `own-session-unseen` and each flow `own-session-outside-record`.

**The rule for a break (ruling 1 on #627, ruling 7 on #628, D3's B3).** The node bounds a backlog record's clock error only by what it measured itself: the length of the break on its own clock.

- **"Break" and "backlog record" are C13's terms, defined in CH-39,** and #628 uses them as defined there. The bound is **`backlog_tie_s`: 60 s by default, at most 120 s,** a node setting (recorded ruling 2 (#628, comment 6803459738); CH-39). A break runs, on the node's monotonic clock, from the last accepted frame of a sensor's connection (CH-44) to the accepted hello of the next; after a node start, the first break is unknown and counts as longer than `backlog_tie_s` (CH-39).
- **Per outage, not per connection** (CH-39). The backlog of a break is every record the node reads from the break's end until it has read through the `newest_seq` of the first hello after the break whose `current` is true (CH-7). So every old run flushed after an outage, on however many connections (CH-16), is judged by the same break. Records numbered after the break ended but read before the backlog is through count as backlog too: it errs on the side of untied. **A second break that begins before a backlog is through** extends that backlog, and its records are judged by the **longest** break of the unfinished backlog, not by the latest, so a short second break never gives ties to records that a long first break left untied (CH-39).
- **After a break longer than `backlog_tie_s`, no backlog record takes a tie.** Each is committed untied with `node.backlog: true`, the node marks each `collection.run` whose window overlaps the break (CH-39), and their `own-session-unseen` and `own-session-outside-record` alerts are aggregated under one key for each rule and sensor, `asset_id` `sensor-records-late`, naming up to 8 assets and counting the rest, as §1.5 does for skew. **An outage raises one alert per rule and window, and not one for each session.** The key shares its name with the alarm `sensor-records-late` (CH-23) and not its meaning: after a break of 61 to 180 s the alerts come under the key while the alarm, which `late_bound_s` raises, does not stand (D4's note on #633; the name is CH-47's, and D4 decides whether to rename the key). A flow that matches no session at all keeps its own key, as in §1.5.
- **After a break at or below `backlog_tie_s`, a backlog record takes a tie only if its own clock agrees with the node's** (D3's change 1 on `4d03075a`, adopted in D1's comment "Round two at `873c0eb5`" on #627, 6803089547; CH-39): its `made_utc` brought to the node's clock, `made_utc + E` with CH-22's sign (§1.5), must fall between the start and the end of the break on the node's wall clock, within `max_skew_ms` (2,000 ms). A record outside that is committed untied with `node.backlog: true`, under the same `sensor-records-late` key. So a clock moved and put back inside a short break ties nothing that the break could not have produced. The node keeps the wall time beside the monotonic time of both instants. **A break whose length on the wall clock differs from its length on the monotonic clock by more than `max_skew_ms` is judged as longer than `backlog_tie_s`** (SL-17 at #641's `c0cf1ecc`): a step left inside a short break puts the break's two ends on two clocks while E, taken on the new connection, puts the record on the new one, so where one source steps the sensor's clock with the node's a record could tie to a session that ran before its flow (with the node's clock stepped alone, E on the new connection is about the step and §1.5 already makes no tie: D6's B2 on #641 at `c0cf1ecc`). It is §1.5's rule on the two clocks applied to the break's two ends, and that rule also covers a step found after the hello while the backlog is read (D5's point on #641 at `c0cf1ecc`). What stays is a step made and undone between two ticks, or records the engine writes after the clock is put back, which D3 records in AP-35.
- **Why 60 s.** It is `SensorUnreachableAfter`, so every break whose backlog loses its ties has already raised `sensor-unreachable`, while a single reconnect (back-off 1 s, handshake and hello at most 10 s each) keeps its ties, under the `made_utc` check. CH-39 gives the same figure, and its 120 s ceiling: above about 60 s a spoofed session can reach a neighbouring scheduled run's window (D3).
- **This changes what brief item 5 asked.** After a break longer than 60 s, the sessions in it do alert, once each window under the aggregated key, instead of not at all. That is D1's fail-closed choice, and D5 agrees with it: the alternative ties on a clock nobody measured.

**The late index (part (e) of the hold), for what remains.** A record can still arrive after its session's result with no break: the engine may write a connection's record only at its end (A6 on #627), and a break of at most `backlog_tie_s` keeps ties, under the `made_utc` check. **Requirement, not built:**

- **Only a session released with no flow tied enters the index** (D3's L2 (i)), with invocation, asset, endpoint, transport, local address, connected, start and end.
- **An entry ties one flow and is then spent**, under `Result`'s rules: the 5-tuple for a 0.9 result, the first flow in the window for a 0.8 result, a failed dial's window. So a second flow on a tied session's key, or a second flow in a 0.8 window, commits untied and alerts, as on `main` (AP-35). If the tied flow is not ended, it joins the open table (part (d)).
- **Horizon: 15 minutes on the node's clock** after the session's end: `MaxFlowIdleS` (600 s), plus `backlog_tie_s` at its 120 s ceiling, plus `g`, plus room for the engine's own pipeline, which E-D4-03 measures (**unmeasured**). Past it a record commits untied and alerts, counted `late_untied` (`horizon`).
- **Bound: `HoldLateEntries` = 1,024,** oldest out first, counted `late_untied` (`evicted`), with `sensor-late-index-short` raised once a stretch when an entry is evicted inside its horizon. The scheduler runs one run at a time, so 15 minutes hold far fewer than 1,024 sessions at any schedule `MinIntervalSeconds` allows unless runs last under a second. Each entry is about 200 bytes (**unmeasured**), so under 256 KiB.
- **It is memory only.** Its source is `collection.run`, and it starts empty at each start. Entries rebuilt at a start were recorded before it, so under §1.5's rule on the two clocks they tie nothing; whether to keep the rebuild is D5's (SL-17 at #641's `50a67730`). **D5 drops the rebuild,** which read the health stream back 15 minutes at a start, as `lastDropCounts` reads (`sensor.go`), and counted `evicted` a session pruned before that. A rebuilt entry could only refuse a tie, and that refusal would commit a flow that matches it with `node.skewed: true` under `clock-skew`, aggregated, for a move of a clock that may never have happened. With no entry the flow commits untied under its own key and alerts, which fails closed and names it rightly, and the start reads nothing more.

**Why the detector needs no further change, and when it would.** It judges a session only once its capture clock passes the end plus `g` (`judge`). The late index works if a flow's record that starts in a session's window is read before any record of a flow that starts after the window plus `g`. **That is stated in 0.2 and marked [S2]** (difference 2 on #627): C13 0.1 already asks a flow's `start` at its first frame. If E-D4-03 shows that the engine writes only at a connection's end, the detector judges a session once its clock passes the end plus `g` plus `flow_idle_s` (D4's fallback), which delays `own-session-unseen` only.

**Lost records** (difference 3 on #627; CH-40, CH-46). The agent declares the capture spans of records it evicted or could not read before the node acknowledged them, as `lost` in `sensor-hello` and `tick`, and as `prior.lost` for whole runs and discarded generations: the run's full set, at most 8 disjoint spans. **The node follows CH-40:**

- **It keeps the run's spans durably, in the cursor, in the transaction that accepts them** (SL-31, D3's ruling (b) in #641; CH-40, CH-18; D6 on `e2ff17c7`). The spans the node holds for a run, those of `prior.lost` included, each as stated and with its fixed cut end, are saved in the sensor database's cursor (§1.4) **in the same `SaveSourceCursor` that raises D for them,** or in row 6's save for `prior.lost`, before the message that carried them is acted on. They are never kept apart from D, so they cannot fall behind it, and a rename lost at a power cut cannot touch them. `sensorrun.json` holds no span. They change only with an eviction, so the sensor cannot make the node save beyond what a rise in D already allows. After a node restart, a resume's unchanged `lost` list is therefore not new, and passes. *Why not the sensor's word after a loss of the node's own* (D3's ruling against options (a) and (c)): a span the node never saw rise would go unchecked, and a span it had cut would be cut again at the restart.
- **Each span's gap is owed from that transaction** (SL-31). The span is saved with a mark that its `lost` gap is owed. The mark is cleared only by a later cursor save made after the gap's `sensor.coverage-gap` event has committed (the marked span is the gap's durable hold; §1.9 item 5). After a restart the node writes the gap of every span still marked, and raises `sensor-records-lost`. A kill between the two writes gives the gap twice, and never not at all.
- **It checks each span.** **An impossible span closes the connection:** a span whose `from_utc` is after its `to_utc` closes it (`tick`, or `hello` for a span in a hello). **A span that widens with no rise closes it the same way:** a span that is new, or wider than the spans the node holds for the run, must come with a rise in that run's `drops_channel` **above Ds, the D at which the run's spans were last accepted** (§1.4), or, for `prior.lost`, a `prior.records_lost` above 0. **Why Ds and not D** (D5's N8 on #641): a heartbeat carries `drops_channel` and no spans (CH-15), so a heartbeat made after an eviction and read before the tick that declares its span raises D first; judged against D, that tick would show no rise and close an honest sensor. Ds moves only when spans are accepted, so the rise that pays for a span is counted once. **A span that reaches past the present is cut once, and not closed** (CH-40 of `docs/phase4/D4-c13-0.2-proposal.md` (#627), from D6 and D3 on round three, recorded ruling 4 (#628, comment 6803459738) and D1's ruling on CH-40 (#627, comment 6803460354); the sensor's clock may have been stepped back since, CH-21):
  - **The node holds each span twice: as the sensor stated it, and with its cut end.** Both are in the cursor.
  - **The cut end is fixed once,** when the span is accepted as new or widened, at the earliest of three times: the span's stated `to_utc`; the message's `sensor_utc`; and **the node's own receipt time plus `max_skew_ms`** (2,000 ms). The third term does not use E, because E is itself derived from `sensor_utc`, the sensor's word (D3's correction, recorded ruling 4 (#628, comment 6803459738), which replaces the earlier "receipt time less E"). *Example:* the node receives the message at `12:00:10`, so the third term is `12:00:12`; a span stated to end at `12:05:00` in a message whose `sensor_utc` is `12:03:00` is cut at `12:00:12`. A cut is counted under `spans_cut`, and the `lost` gap and the alarm below are raised as for any new span. **A span whose window is empty after the cut** (its `from_utc` at or after the cut end) writes no `node.lost` entry.
  - **Later messages are compared with the span as stated, never with the cut one.** An unchanged stated span is not new and not wider, so an honest sensor is not closed a tick later, nor cut or counted again. A span stated wider needs a rise in the counters, as any widening does, and its new part gets a cut end of its own, fixed once in the same way. **One cut end for each span, so the cursor has a fixed size** (D5's note on #641; stated in CH-40 under P4-4-L6, §1.11 row 8): the node keeps one degraded window for each span, from its stated `from_utc` to its cut end. A part added before `from_utc` extends the window back. A part added after the stated `to_utc` extends the window to that part's own cut end only when the window was not cut (its cut end equals the stated `to_utc`), so the two are contiguous; when the window was cut, the added part's window is not kept, and only the gap and the alarm are raised for it. The window is therefore never wider than CH-40's two pieces, and a part left out can only make the own-session check judge more, which fails loud. The cut end never moves forward otherwise, so a span cannot follow time and keep `own-session-unseen` off for good. `node.lost` and the detector's degraded interval use the cut end.
  - Test: `TestLostSpanCutOnce` (a span reaching an hour ahead, repeated unchanged in later ticks: cut once at the receipt time plus 2,000 ms, connection open, the degraded interval ends at the cut; a `sensor_utc` and an E both stated an hour ahead do not move the cut; a span starting after the cut writes no `node.lost`; controls: the cut at the receipt time less E, which an hour-ahead `sensor_utc` moves; the cut recomputed at each message, which lets it follow time, and a comparison with the cut span, which closes on the next tick).
- **It shows each loss.** Every new or widened span opens a **coverage gap with cause `lost`**, under the `lost` gaps' own budget (§1.9 item 5), and raises **`sensor-records-lost`**, a status alarm for the stretch.
- **It marks it.** On the next observation of that run that it stores, the node sets `node.lost`, **a list of at most 8 `{from_utc, to_utc}`**; a new run's `prior.lost` goes on the first observation of the new run.
- The detector reads each span as a degraded interval, so a session inside a declared loss raises no false `own-session-unseen` (`covers`, `own.go`). If no observation of the run follows, the mark is not placed, and such a session may alert: it fails loud (CH-40). `sensor-gap-undeclared` and the coverage gap keep the loss loud.

**Late records** (CH-23): a record later than `late_bound_s` is stored with `node.late: true`, counted, and raises `sensor-records-late` once for each connection that carries one, under the cap of §1.9 item 5. Lateness uses E. `late` is the sensor's own word (`made_utc`), so it never decides a tie: the break rule above does.

**The buffer the sensor must cover** (difference 9 on #627): `buffer_bytes` at least the site's record rate times `outage_hours`, a sizing rule that **cannot be checked until a station's rate is measured.** The node shows `buffered_bytes` against `buffer_bytes` from each tick, and raises `sensor-buffer-filling` once a stretch past three quarters. **`sensor-buffer-small`** stands while a sensor's hello states a `buffer_bytes` below the 256 MiB site floor (CH-13 row 9, CH-20): a buffer that small cannot be caught by `sensor-buffer-filling`, since no tick arrives during the outage that fills it.

**Tests.** `TestBreakOverBoundNoTie` (a break of 61 s on the test clock: the backlog's flows commit untied and alert under `sensor-records-late`; control: tied); `TestBreakUnderBoundTies`; `TestShortBreakMadeUtcCheck` (a clock set back 10 minutes inside a 30 s break: its backlog records are untied; control: the check removed, which ties them, D3); `TestShortBreakNodeStepNoTie` (a 40 s break, `tie_grace_s` 1 s, the test source's clock and the node's stepped back 30 s together 10 s into it, a failing dial to a relay 5 s into the break, and a backlog record made 35 s into it, of a flow from the node's address to that relay, stamped 5 s: committed untied with `node.backlog: true` and no `node.skewed`, alerting under `sensor-records-late`; control: a node with no rule on the two clocks, which judges the break by its monotonic length alone and ties it; second case, D5's: a 40 s break with no step in it, both clocks stepped back 30 s together 1 s after the hello, the backlog read for 90 s, a failing dial to the relay 24 s after the step for 2 s, and a backlog record made 35 s into the break, of a flow from the node's address to that relay, stamped 35 s, read 70 s after the step: untied, with no `node.skewed`, under `sensor-records-late`; control: the same node, which ties it; in both cases the capture floor refuses the record too, so each pins the break's ends through the key: a node with no rule on the break's ends commits it with `node.skewed: true` under `clock-skew` (D6's N5 on #641 at `50a67730`)); `TestFirstBreakAfterStartUnknown`; `TestBacklogEndsAtCurrentRun`; `TestLateIndexTiesOnce` (a second flow on a tied session's key alerts; control: the entry not spent); `TestLateIndexOnlyUntiedSessions`; `TestLateRecordTied` (control: no index, untied); `TestLateIndexEmptyAtStart` (a session released with no flow tied before a stop, and after the start, with no break left to judge, a flow from the node's address that matches it: committed untied under its own key, without `node.skewed`; control: a node that rebuilds the index at a start, which commits it under `clock-skew`); `TestLatePastHorizon`; `TestLateIndexBound`; `TestLostIntervalNoOwnSessionUnseen` (control: no `node.lost`, which alerts); `TestLostSpanChecks` (a reversed span, and a new span with no rise in `drops_channel`, each closing; a span that ends after the message's `sensor_utc` cut, not closed; control: each accepted unchecked); `TestLostSpansListOfEight`; `TestLostSpansSurviveRestart` (a node restart, then a resume with unchanged spans: accepted; controls: spans in memory only, and spans in `sensorrun.json` with that file replaced by an older copy, each of which closes every dial); `TestLostSpanRiseJudgedAgainstDs` (a heartbeat made after an eviction read before the tick that declares its span: the tick is accepted; control: the rise judged against D, which closes it); `TestLostSpanOneCutEnd` (a cut span widened after its stated end: the window is unchanged, the gap and alarm are raised; an uncut span widened: the window reaches the new cut end; control: the window taken to the new part's cut end in both, which covers the time between the first cut and the stated end); `TestLostSpanCutNotClosed` (a span ending after `sensor_utc`: counted under `spans_cut`, gap and alarm raised, connection open; control: closed); `TestLostSpanCutOnce`; `TestPriorDurableUntilEvent` (killed after the hello and before `sensor.run-loss` commits: the event is written at restart, carrying the run's generation, counter and `run_id`; control: `prior` in memory only); `TestBufferSmallAlarm`. On the rig: the link cut for 30 s across one run (ties kept), and for 10 minutes across 5 runs (one aggregated alert per rule and window, naming the asset).

## 1.7 Retiring and replacing a sensor (brief item 6)

- **A replacement keeps its `sensor_id`.** A new host, a re-image or a new key is a new enrolment of the same `sensor_id` at the next generation (SL-16, SL-R6). Its streams, baseline and detector carry on, and the open table clears at the new run. Nothing is retired.
- **Retiring is for a `sensor_id` withdrawn:** a LAN no longer watched, or a revoked sensor not replaced. Today a sensor taken out of the configuration leaves its database on disk, unopened, never pruned and never deleted (Part 2 §5, `layout.go`).
- **Requirement, not built (ruling 12, D6's B6).** At the first start with a `sensor_id` newly in `retired`, the sensor manager, in order:
  1. opens its database as any sensor database (§1.9 item 1), with no dialer and no link;
  2. releases everything its hold holds, untied, and commits it;
  3. runs its detector to the observation stream's head, commits the alerts, and stores a last checkpoint;
  4. only then adds the consumer `node-retired`, which confirms the observation stream through the head, so nothing unjudged is pruned.
  - Status shows the drain. If a step cannot finish (its database will not open, or a commit fails), the manager tries again at each tick and does not confirm. After 24 hours it writes a health event that **declares what it drops,** the seq range and the count, and only then confirms. A retired sensor's records are never pruned unjudged without that event.
  - The alert stream keeps its forward targets, or the retention consumer (`netretain`, `alert_retention_days`) where there is none. Network baselines are evidence and are never removed (C5 0.9). Once both streams are empty, status says so, and the administrator removes the entry; the database then stays on disk unopened.
- **Tests.** `TestRetiredSensorDrained` (held records committed, every observation judged, then pruned; control: the design of the first text, which prunes unjudged observations and strands held ones); `TestRetiredDrainStuckDeclares`; `TestRetiredSensorNotDialled`.

## 1.8 `sensor-blind`, reachability and coverage unknown (brief item 7)

- **`sensor-unreachable`** (ruling 3 on #627; CH-44): only an **accepted** frame resets its timer: an accepted tick that is not early, or a heartbeat or record taken, on a connection whose hello was accepted. A refused record, a failed hello, an early tick, an unknown type or a connect does not (AP-36 (2), D4-73). Code: `tickSensor`'s timer (`sensor.go`), moved to the manager.
- **`sensor-blind`** (difference 6 on #627), per interface (`interfaces[]`, CH-31): an interface whose `packets` has not risen across the heartbeats of the last `blind_after_s`, or whose `carrier` is `down`, is blind, judged from heartbeats that are not late. `sensor-blind` is raised when **any** interface is blind, naming it; a coverage gap with cause `blind` opens when **every** interface is. The counters run per run, so the comparison starts again at a new run.
- **`blind_after_s`:** a node setting, 300 s by default (difference 6 on #627), 60 s to 3,600 s. **Assumption** (A7 on #627): a mirrored station LAN carries something within the bound. D2's N-6 on #627 documents traffic every few seconds wherever a SEL DAN is mirrored, which supports a shorter setting there. With the manager's 1 s drive the alarm comes within the bound and one second.
- **Coverage unknown** (ruling 3 on #627, CH-44, D3's note 1): while accepted ticks arrive and no heartbeat that is not late has come for 60 s, as while a backlog drains, neither `sensor-unreachable` nor `sensor-blind` can stand. Status shows `coverage: unknown`, and **`sensor-no-heartbeat`** is raised once a stretch, the name and figure of CH-44 in `docs/phase4/D4-c13-0.2-proposal.md` (#627) (D6's B5 on #627: a tail that wedges the sensor shows here). A flat interface count seen only in late heartbeats is recorded afterwards as a past blind stretch, in one `sensor.past-blind` health event under the cap (CH-44).
- **The heartbeat baselines are durable** (D5's B1 on #634). `sensor-transmitted` and `sensor-egress-absent` need what the node took earlier in the run, and heartbeats are never stored, and a resume sends nothing at or below `taken`. So the cursor (§1.4) keeps, for the cursor's run, the last `frames_outgoing`, `drops_holder`, `frames_overlong` and `drops_driver` taken and whether `egress_check` was carried, saved before a heartbeat that changes them is acted on, which is rare. A new run starts with none.
  - **Every heartbeat taken is judged, late ones included.** CH-23 takes late heartbeats out of the live checks (skew, blind) only.
  - **With no baseline held for the run, any non-zero `frames_outgoing` raises `sensor-transmitted`.** That covers the first heartbeat of a run, a run whose first heartbeats were evicted (declared in `drops_channel` and `lost`) or fall in a gap, and a sensor database lost or unread. With a baseline, any rise raises it. So a transmit is never missed for want of a comparison, and is at worst raised again for one already raised, which fails loud.
  - **The run's first heartbeat seen from its start sets the `egress_check` baseline,** carried or not, and raises nothing if it omits the field (SL-26 §11.3: a sensor that cannot read the rule leaves the field out from the start of the run, and that raises no alarm; status shows the egress state as `unknown`). "Seen from its start" is CH-27's: the node accepted the run as a new run (CH-13 row 6) and has read every `seq` of it below that heartbeat with no declared loss.
  - **With no baseline held for the run and a heartbeat that is not the run's first seen from its start** (a lost sensor database, a cursor that cannot be read, or a run whose first heartbeats were evicted, refused or in a gap), **a heartbeat that omits `egress_check` raises `sensor-egress-absent`**, until a new run shows from its start whether the field is carried. The node then cannot tell a field dropped within the run from one never carried (CH-27). This is worded by the baseline held, not by the run file (D5's N2 on #641). The first text here left out the from-start case, and read literally raised the alarm at every run of a sensor that cannot read the rule (D4's item (b) on #645, C13 §17).
  - **A rise of `drops_driver`, `drops_holder` or `frames_overlong` against its baseline is a capture loss,** named in `sensor.counters`' `capture_loss` detail (C4 0.4; `drops_driver` by D1's ruling on #645 that `capture_loss` includes it). **One rule for all three** (D1's ruling 6840188059 on #645): with no baseline held, the run total taken counts as the rise, and a run total of 0 is no loss. That can count a loss twice after a lost database, stated; it never leaves a loss uncounted. D1's earlier form for `drops_driver` alone (with no baseline held, the first heartbeat sets it and no loss is computed from it; comment 6839936385) is withdrawn, because frames dropped before a node restart would never be counted.
  - **`sensor-transmitted` stays raised across a loss of the node's own** (D1's alarm half in D4-90; ruling 3 on #633 and #634). Its standing state is in `sensorrun.json`, so with that file unreadable, lost or behind (§1.1), **or not comparable with that sensor's database because the database is lost, absent, unread or without a cursor** (SL-30 and §11.3 at #641's `467c1dd6`; D6's B4 on #641: on a node with one sensor nothing else can show a stale file to be behind), while `sensors.json` holds a live enrolment, the node raises it until an administrator clears it. A loss never clears it silently.
- **Tests.** `TestUnreachableOnlyAcceptedFrame` (control: any frame resets, which hides a sensor whose every hello fails); `TestSensorBlindAnyInterface` (control: blind only when all are); `TestSensorBlindIgnoresLate`; `TestCoverageUnknownDuringBacklog`; `TestPastBlindStretchRecorded`; `TestTransmittedAtFirstHeartbeat` (C13's W-5 start leg: a frame sent out of a monitoring interface after the holder opens its socket and before the run's first heartbeat makes that heartbeat's `frames_outgoing` non-zero, and `sensor-transmitted` is raised; control: no frame sent gives 0 and raises nothing; and a node that raises only on a rise misses the first case); `TestEgressDroppedWithinRun` (a run whose heartbeats carry `egress_check` `ok` and then leave it out: `sensor-egress-absent` is raised; control: a node that reads a dropped field as "not carried", which stays silent). **D5's B1 legs:** `TestTransmittedAfterNodeRestart` (heartbeats with `frames_outgoing` 0 taken, the node restarted, then one with 1: the alarm is raised); `TestTransmittedFirstHeartbeatEvicted` (a run's first heartbeat, carrying 1, evicted and declared; the first heartbeat the node takes also carries 1: the alarm is raised); `TestEgressDroppedAfterNodeRestart` (a field carried, the node restarted, then left out: the alarm is raised); the control for all three is a node that compares within a connection only, which stays silent. `TestEgressNeverCarriedNoAlarm` (SL-26 §11.3's quiet case and CH-27's exception: a run whose every heartbeat omits `egress_check`, taken from its start: no `sensor-egress-absent`, and status shows `unknown`; the same run with its first heartbeat evicted and declared: the alarm is raised; control: a node that raises whenever it holds no baseline, which alarms in the first case). **`TestCaptureLossAcrossRestart`** (C13's W-8 (f) and (g), D1's one rule): heartbeats with `drops_driver` 5 taken, the node restarted, then one with 9: `sensor.counters` carries `diff.drops_driver` 4 and `capture_loss` names `drops_driver`; the same for `drops_holder` and `frames_overlong`; a run taken with no baseline held whose first heartbeat taken carries `drops_driver` 7: `diff.drops_driver` 7 and `capture_loss` names it; and one carrying 0 for all three: no `capture_loss`. *Controls:* a node that keeps no durable baseline for `drops_driver`, which after the restart reports 9 or nothing; the withdrawn form, which computes no loss from the first heartbeat and reports nothing for the 7; and a node that reports a loss for a run total of 0. On the rig: K-2 and K-3 of #627.

## 1.9 A sensor never stops SER or Syslog collection or the node's start (brief item 8)

Each finding of §1.0, and what closes it. **D1's ruling on #628 (comment 6803782160): every row below is closed, with its test, in the first brief of the third part, before any dialer or other caller of `ServeSensor` or `sensorlink.Link.Serve` merges, and before any release.**

### 1. The sensor store set (ruling 8; F5, F6, F7, F9, F10)

**The mechanism D5 chooses: the sensor databases are never in `Runtime.dbs`.** They form a separate set, owned by the sensor manager, which opens, resumes, prunes and closes them on its own goroutine, and records their mode changes. **Its lock (`sensorStores.mu`) is held only to read or swap a handle, never across a store call** (D6's item 2 on `4d03075a`):

- **The accessor never waits.** It takes the lock to read the handle and add a reference, and returns at once: the handle, or "not open".
- **`Resume`, prune and open run with the lock released,** as `Tick`'s `OpenBusy` branch already does on `main`. Before a `Resume`, the manager swaps the handle out under the lock, so the accessor returns "not open" for that database while it runs, and swaps it back after.
- **A handle is closed only when no caller holds a reference to it,** so no reader's call is cut by a close.

So:

- `r.state`, the lock that `view` takes for every `Log`, `Store` and `Mode` call of the scheduler and the Syslog intake, is never held over a sensor database: `Tick`'s `Resume` loop iterates `r.dbs` only (F5).
- `pruneMu` and `Runtime.Prune` cover `r.dbs` only, so `cannotKeep`'s `Prune` never waits on a network database (F6). The manager prunes its own set after each drive, with the same `pruneDB` passes.
- `Start` opens `r.dbs` only. A sensor database is opened by the manager after `Start` returns; one that will not open, adopt or resume is an alarm (`sensor-store-not-open`), stays closed, and is tried again at each manager tick, under `StartupDeadline`, never a failed start (F7).
- The configuration database stays shared, as it is today: the hold's records and the detector's checkpoints are writes to it, each one call under the store's own mutex and item 3's ceiling (`saveIntake`), as configuration reads' writes are. The audit and evidence databases are touched only by single reads and writes, each under its own call (the fold in pages of 64; cursor saves at most once a minute; the detector's paged reads of the health stream).
- `Runtime.Log` and `Runtime.Store` for a sensor stream go through the manager's accessor; `Runtime.Collecting` (no product caller) does not count the sensor set.
- **The forwarder (F10).** `NewForwarding` gives each target a second session that carries the sensor's streams only, apart from the session that carries every other stream. A sensor database that is not open then ends that second session's attempt, and the session for SER, Syslog, audit and health events goes on. It costs a second connection to each target. `Forwarding.Check` reads each stream through the accessor, which never waits.
- **Disk space.** The volume rule (`deploy/README.md`) counts the sensor set as it counts every database: each class's quota times `DiskBytesPerQuotaByte`, with each database's two reserves (D6).
- **Mode changes (F9):** a sensor database's `store.mode_change` audit record and its health event are written at most once an hour for each database, the rest counted and carried in the next record's detail; the health event is under the ceiling.

**Code changed:** `runtime.go` (`Start`, `startDB`, `Tick`, `Log`, `Store`, `Close`), `prune.go`, `layout.go` (`Layout` returns the sensor set apart), and a new `platform/node/sensormgr.go`. **Tests**, each with a control: `TestSensorDrainOnlyDoesNotStallSyslog` (a sensor database held drain-only with a slow-prune seam while Syslog arrives, a run is due and a target forwards SER events: Syslog commits, the run is sent within 2 s, SER events reach the target, and `Tick` returns; controls: `main`'s `Tick`, and the manager's lock held across `Resume`); `TestSensorStoreNotOpenForwardingGoesOn` (a sensor database not open while SER events forward: they reach the target; control: one session for every stream, `main`'s `NewForwarding`); `TestCannotKeepPrunesEvidenceOnly` (adapter-evidence near its limit and a network class over target, with a slow prune seam; control: `main`'s `Prune`); `TestSensorStoreFailsOpenNodeStarts` (a network database whose first read fails: the node starts and stores a run; control: `main`'s `startDB`); `TestSensorModeChangeCapped` (control: uncapped).

### 2. The start (F2)

`startSensor`'s error no longer fails `Start`. The node starts without that sensor's intake, raises `sensor-intake-not-started` and tries again at each manager tick. **Said plainly (D6):** while the intake is not started, `repairIntake` does not run, so a run whose release committed before a crash has no `collection.run` until it does. Shape checks stay load failures (§1.2). Test: `TestHoldRecordUnreadableNodeStarts` (a hold record that cannot be read: the node starts and stores a run; control: `main`'s `Start`).

### 3. The detector's drive (ruling 10; F3; D4's note, D6's B4)

- The sensor manager drives the detector **about once a second**, with at most 1,000 observations or 200 ms a drive, yielding between drives (**unmeasured**; the figures only keep one drive short). No budget a minute caps the rate: a backlog is worked off as fast as the drives allow.
- **The checkpoint at most once every 60 s,** and at a clean stop, since a worst-case checkpoint is 1,354,752 bytes.
- **The lag alarm: `network-detector-lag`** when the oldest observation the detector has not read was committed more than **60 s** ago on the node's clock, raised once a stretch, cleared below 30 s, with the lag in status. 60 s is one checkpoint interval, and C13 §7.3 means a first alert to be committed "at once", which a minute of lag already departs from. A flood therefore delays alerts visibly, never silently.
- A bounded drive keeps the existing ordering rule: no session is handed over after observations past its `after_seq`, and `nd.health` passes only runs handed over (D4).
- `Runtime.Tick` takes the manager's status snapshot without waiting, and does nothing else for the sensor. The final drop-count write of `Close` moves to the manager's stop.
- **Tests:** `TestTickReturnsWithSlowDetector` (a slow detector seam: `Tick` returns and the evidence database resumes; control: `tickSensor` inside `Tick`); `TestDetectorLagAlarm` (control: no alarm, where the backlog grows silently); and SL-T13 leg (a)'s pass condition from D6's plan §5: the labelled case sent in the middle of the flood has its alert stored, with the latency reported against the node's clock, **and when that latency is above 60 s, `network-detector-lag` stood** (D6).

### 4. The run path and the link's lock (ruling 11; F4; D6's B5, D3's condition)

- **Fairness:** the scheduler goes first. A count of waiting scheduler calls is kept beside `Link.cmu`; the read loop releases the lock after each record and, while a scheduler call waits, does not take it again until that call has had it. So a scheduler call waits for at most the one record in commit.
- **`HoldLockWait` = 2 s,** the most any scheduler call waits (**unmeasured**: D6 cites F-016's commits held 3 s and up to 37.9 s on the hosted Windows runner, so on a slow disk it can trip).
- **Past it, the session is kept, marked, and judged:** `Dispatch` holds nothing (the run's flows commit untied); `Result` gives `collection.run` the session's facts with `hold: timeout` and `after_seq` read without the lock, so the detector still judges the session. Its own-session alerts are aggregated under the key `hold-timeout`, naming assets up to 8, as §1.5 does (D3's condition: a cap that protects collection must not switch off the independent check).
- **An alarm, not only a counter:** `network-session-not-tied` once a stretch, when `dispatch_timeouts` or `result_timeouts` rises.
- **Tests:** SL-T13 leg (a) asserts `dispatch_timeouts` and `result_timeouts` stay 0 on the rig, with an unfair lock as the control; `TestResultTimeoutKeepsSession` (control: the session dropped, as the first text had it).

### 5. The platform-class ceiling, in bytes (ruling 9; F1; D6's B2)

**Collection's need is reserved first. The sensor's share is worked out from its caps. The quota must hold both.**

- **Each health record is at most 4,096 bytes stored** (**unmeasured**: the node builds each sensor event to render in at most 2,048 octets, as `TestSensorDropEventFits` pins for `intake.drop-counted`, and allows as much again for the C4 envelope and the store). A size test pins it for every sensor event, **and for `collection.run` and `collection.missed` with the per-sensor keys of §1.2** (D6).
- **Collection's need over the outage:** `C = R × 2 × 4,096 × H` bytes, where `R` is the schedule's runs an hour (the sum of 3,600 / `every_seconds` over its entries), 2 is the run's own records (its `collection.*` record and a `collection.missed`), and `H` is `outage_hours`. **`C` is a lower bound** (`records-auditor`, D6): it counts collection's run records only. Other writers to the platform class are not counted by `C` or `S`: the node's own databases' store alarms and mode changes, forwarding's and the Syslog intake's health events, and the node's other health events (`runtime.go` `health`, `modeHealth`, `storeAlarm`). They write there today with no floor. So `C` reserves collection's own records against the sensor, not against every writer.
- **The sensor's caps, for each sensor, an hour:**
  - `intake.drop-counted` for two classes, at most once every 10 minutes each (from once a minute: the totals are now durable in the cursor, §1.4): 12;
  - `sensor.coverage-gap` with cause `lost`: 4, **a budget of its own**, so that link drops cannot delay a declared loss (D4-90's gap rule; D4's B4 on #633);
  - `sensor.coverage-gap` of every other cause: 4, **its own budget, never behind another event** (D6);
  - a pool of 24 for every other sensor event, **each name at most 4 an hour, and `sensor.counters` also no sooner than 10 minutes after its last** (CH-45; so at most 4 an hour, D5's N6 on #634): the events of the alarms `sensor-clock-skew`, `sensor-blind`, `sensor-no-heartbeat`, `sensor-gap-undeclared`, `sensor-records-late`, `sensor-records-lost`, `sensor-buffer-filling`, `sensor-buffer-small`, `sensor-coverage-mismatch`, `sensor-pinned-by-console`, `sensor-identity`, `sensor-egress-absent`, `sensor-dial-limit`, `sensor-transmitted`, `sensor-link-key`, `sensor-state-unread`, `sensor-enrolment-expiring`, `sensor-enrolment-expired`, `network-detector-lag` and `network-session-not-tied`; and the events `sensor.past-blind`, `sensor.run-loss`, `sensor.counters`, `sensor.redial-granted` and `sensor.revocation-applied`, and the sensor database's store alarms. **This is one list with CH-45's pool** (ruling 4 on #633 and #634 (comment 6805936000)). **When the pool is spent, what waits:** `sensor.counters` first, then `sensor.run-loss`, then the alarms' events, and `sensor.redial-granted` and `sensor.revocation-applied` last, which are held durably in `sensorrun.json` and written first when room returns; a waiting event is merged into the next of its name, and counted in `health_held`. **The sensor alarms outside this list write no health event:** `sensor-unreachable`, `sensor-position-not-saved`, `sensor-failed`, `sensor-state-not-durable`, `sensor-revoke-pending`, `sensor-not-enrolled`, `sensor-address-is-asset`, `sensor-platform-floor`, `sensor-store-not-open`, `sensor-intake-not-started`, `sensor-late-index-short` and `sensor-not-built` are status and the OS log only, as `Status.Alarm` writes on `main`. Each interval they leave unwatched is a coverage gap below (D5's N7 on #634);
  - mode changes, at most 1 for each database (item 1);
  - so 45 records an hour for a sensor, and 3 for a retired one (its mode change and 2 store alarms).
- **The sensor's share:** `S = (45 × sensors + 3 × retired) × 4,096 × H` bytes.
- **No refusal at load** (recorded ruling 1 (#628, comment 6803459738), D3's change 3, D6's item 1). When `store.quotas.platform` is below `C + S`, the node starts, **the sensor is not started** (no intake, no dial), and `sensor-platform-floor` stands, naming both terms. Adding a schedule entry raises `R`, so this case can arise with no change to the sensor, which is why it must not refuse a start. **This brief adds no check where there is no `network_sensor` section.** Recorded ruling 1 keeps collection's own need not fitting for the existing configuration floor, and adds no new floor when no sensor is configured; `main` has no platform floor today. A configuration check run before deployment may still report either case as an error.
- **The ceiling, at run time:** a sensor event is written only while the platform class holds less than `quota − C` bytes after it. So collection's own `C` is always left against the sensor, whatever the upstream does.
- **Worked example** (arithmetic, not a measurement): one sensor, no retired one, `H` = 24, and a schedule of 10 entries every 600 s (`R` = 60). `C` = 60 × 2 × 4,096 × 24 = 11,796,480 bytes (11.25 MiB). `S` = 45 × 4,096 × 24 = 4,423,680 bytes (4.22 MiB). `C + S` = 16,220,160 bytes (15.47 MiB), within the 64 MiB the README's example gives the class (`platform/node/README.md`).
- **Held events and a restart:** an event past the ceiling is not written. A pool event's counts are kept in `sensorrun.json`, written at most once a minute when they change, so a restart finds them and writes one merged record when room returns; with that file lost or behind (§1.1) they are named in status as possibly lost. **A held coverage gap is held in the cursor, not in that file:** a `lost` gap in its span's owed mark (§1.6), and every other gap in W, which does not move past it, or, a gap closed unwatched whose raise of W is saved before its event commits, in the gap held for the floor (below). Status shows `health_held`. A held gap is therefore never dropped silently, by a crash or by a run file that is lost or behind; a crash can lose up to the last minute's held pool counts, stated.
- **Held events are kept as counts and facts, never as built event bytes** (C4 0.4 §3's rollback consequence; D1's ruling on D5's N4, comment 6839936385 on #645). What `sensorrun.json` holds for a pool event is its name, its counts or rises, and the facts its details carry (for `sensor.redial-granted` and `sensor.revocation-applied`, the sensor, generation and times); a held gap is its marks and W in the cursor.
